People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I’ve been feeling like I’m always going from one thing to the next. Like I can’t really just sit idle. With the new setup at work, it’s been harder to cover that up.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s not always obvious, I guess, but my foot’s usually bouncing under the desk, or I’ll be clicking a pen in a meeting without realizing I’m doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah. Not in a big way, I don’t think… but at work, if I hear someone trying to sort out a customer issue, I might jump in with the answer before they’re done talking. I don’t mean to be rude.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to focus on one task for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… if it goes on too long. Like in longer coaching meetings, or when I’m reading a new procedure, I start out okay. But after a few minutes I’ll notice my eyes kept moving over the words, and I didn’t really take much in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness, impulsivity, or difficulty focusing are causing problems for you in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a bit. Mostly at work. It’s started coming up in my reviews—unfinished tickets, little mistakes that slow things down. I’m trying, and I usually do okay with the customers, but… yeah, it’s been noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been this way, even as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Teachers would write that I was bright, but careless, and I had to be reminded to stay on my work. I also talked out of turn some. So… it was there back then too.\n\nDoctor: Were you more active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I was. I don’t remember every detail, but I’ve always had that thing where I’m kind of moving on to the next thing, not really able to just sit and idle. Like if I’m given a few steps to handle something, I’ll do the first part, but then the notes or the follow-up just kind of sit there until someone reminds me.\n\nDoctor: Are your symptoms causing problems for you in your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Not so much with friends or family… at least not in a big way. The main place it’s causing problems is work. It’s started coming up in my reviews—unfinished tickets, little mistakes that slow the team down. I’m trying, and I get along with customers okay, but… yeah, that part’s been noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with focusing and following through on tasks is more of a problem for you than your restlessness and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Yeah, probably the follow-through more than the restlessness. Like, if a supervisor gives me three or four steps for a chat escalation, I’ll usually do the first part… and then the notes or the follow-up just kind of sit there until someone reminds me.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever comment on your behavior or difficulties when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Looking back, it wasn’t just this job. In grade school, teachers would write things like I was bright but careless, or that I talked out of turn. I’m trying, and I get along with customers okay, but… yeah, that part’s been noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with focusing and following through on tasks is more of a problem for you than your restlessness and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Yeah, probably the follow-through more than the restlessness. Like, if a supervisor gives me three or four steps for a chat escalation, I’ll usually do the first part… and then the notes or the follow-up just kind of sit there until someone reminds me.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever comment on your behavior or difficulties when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Looking back, it wasn’t just this job. In grade school, teachers would write things like I was bright but careless, or that I talked out of turn. I remember getting reminded to stay with my work… stuff like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have more trouble with paying attention than with being restless or impulsive?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say the attention part is more of it. Like in longer coaching meetings, or if I’m reading a new procedure, I start out okay, but after a few minutes I realize I’ve been looking at the words and not really taking them in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have more trouble with paying attention than with being restless or impulsive?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think paying attention is more the issue. In those longer coaching meetings, or when I’m reading a new procedure, I’ll start off okay, but after a few minutes I realize my eyes are still moving along and I didn’t really take in much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. (Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n  manifestation: Sitting still through a training video or a slow appointment makes me feel kind of boxed in, like I need to stand up, stretch, or do something with my hands.\n- key: S013\n  name: Quiet_Activity_Difficulty\n  description: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n  manifestation: Quiet stuff that’s supposed to relax me, like reading at home or sitting through a movie with my wife, doesn’t last long before I’m shifting around, commenting, or getting up for something.\n- key: S014\n  name: Constant_Activity\n  description: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n  manifestation: What brings me in is that I feel like I’m always moving from one thing to the next, like I can’t just idle, and the new work setup has made that a lot harder to hide.\n- key: S017\n  name: Waiting_Turn_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n  manifestation: Waiting for my turn in a team huddle or in the help queue makes me antsy, and I catch myself checking the screen over and over or wanting to jump in before I’m called on.\n- key: S018\n  name: Interrupting_Intruding\n  description: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: I don’t mean to be rude, but if I hear coworkers working through a customer problem, I’ll jump in with an answer before they’ve finished or before anyone asked me.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Looking back, this was around long before this job change; Like in longer coaching meetings, or if I’m reading a new procedure, I start out okay, but after a few minutes I realize I’ve been looking at the words and not really taking them in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have more trouble with paying attention than with being restless or impulsive?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think paying attention is more the issue. Not in a big way, I don’t think… but at work, if I hear someone trying to sort out a customer issue, I might jump in with the answer before they’re done talking. Not in a big way, I don’t think… but at work, if I hear someone trying to sort out a customer issue, I might jump in with the answer before they’re done talking. I don’t mean to be rude.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to focus on one task for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… if it goes on too long. Like in longer coaching meetings, or when I’m reading a new procedure, I start out okay. But after a few minutes I’ll notice my eyes kept moving over the words, and I didn’t really take much in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness, impulsivity, or difficulty focusing are causing problems for you in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a bit. Mostly at work. It’s started coming up in my reviews—unfinished tickets, little mistakes that slow things down. I’m trying, and I usually do okay with the customers, but… yeah, it’s been noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been this way, even as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Looking back, it wasn’t just this job. In grade school, teachers used to write things like I was bright but careless, talked out of turn, and needed reminders to stay on my work.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or following instructions when you were in school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. Teachers would write that I was bright, but careless, and I had to be reminded to stay on my work. I also talked out of turn some. So… it was there back then too.\n\nDoctor: Were you more active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I was. I don’t remember every detail, but I’ve always had that thing where I’m kind of moving on to the next thing, not really able to just sit and idle. It’s more obvious now with work, but I think it was there back then too.\n\nDoctor: Have you had difficulty following through on instructions or finishing tasks in your personal life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. Like if I’m given a few steps to handle something, I’ll do the first part, but then the notes or the follow-up just kind of sit there until someone reminds me.\n\nDoctor: Are your symptoms causing problems for you in your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Not so much with friends or family… at least not in a big way. The main place it’s causing problems is work. It’s started coming up in my reviews—unfinished tickets, little mistakes that slow the team down. I’m trying, and I get along with customers okay, but… yeah, that part’s been noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with focusing and following through on tasks is more of a problem for you than your restlessness and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Yeah, probably the follow-through more than the restlessness. Like, if a supervisor gives me three or four steps for a chat escalation, I’ll usually do the first part… and then the notes or the follow-up just kind of sit there until someone reminds me.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever comment on your behavior or difficulties when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Looking back, it wasn’t just this job. In grade school, teachers would write things like I was bright but careless, or that I talked out of turn. I remember getting reminded to stay with my work… stuff like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have more trouble with paying attention than with being restless or impulsive?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say the attention part is more of it. Like in longer coaching meetings, or if I’m reading a new procedure, I start out okay, but after a few minutes I realize I’ve been looking at the words and not really taking them in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have more trouble with paying attention than with being restless or impulsive?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think paying attention is more the issue. In those longer coaching meetings, or when I’m reading a new procedure, I’ll start off okay, but after a few minutes I realize my eyes are still moving along and I didn’t really take in much.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focusing, restlessness, or impulsivity in other areas of your life beyond work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, some. At home mostly. Like if we’re watching a movie, I don’t always just sit through it. I’ll shift around, say something, get up for something… and reading at home doesn’t usually last too long either.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your symptoms are causing you a lot of distress or upset?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say a lot of distress, exactly… but it’s been bothering me. At work it’s started coming up in my reviews — unfinished tickets, little mistakes that slow the team down. I’m trying hard, and I usually do fine with customers, so… yeah, that’s frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with focusing, restlessness, or impulsivity in social situations with friends?\n\nPatient: Not really sure. I mean, with friends I don’t think it’s been a big issue… nothing that stands out to me, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with focusing, restlessness, or impulsivity before you started working?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Looking back, it wasn’t just this job. Even in grade school, teachers would write that I was bright but careless, talked out of turn, needed reminders to stay on my work… that kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Are your symptoms causing problems for you in any of your close relationships?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any major way. I mean, people close to me might get annoyed sometimes, but I can’t say it’s causing real problems.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
